========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I came in because things at work got… honestly, kind of out of control. I’m an industrial production manager, and I went through this stretch where I was suddenly taking on everything—launching one plant redesign project after another, calling suppliers after hours like it was completely normal, rewriting the whole shift plan by myself because I felt like I could just fix it faster than waiting on anyone else.

At the time it felt productive, like I was finally seeing all the problems clearly and could solve them if I just kept pushing. But then it flipped, or caught up with me, I guess. I’d set all these plans in motion, gotten other departments and vendors involved, changed expectations for people on the floor… and then suddenly I couldn’t keep up with the very things I had started. It was like I’d built this huge machine and then couldn’t operate it. That’s what scared me enough to come in.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Very much so. It’s not just stress or being tired from work — it feels heavier than that.

Lately I’ve been waking up in the temporary apartment, and before I even get out of bed there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the whole day is already ruined before I’ve even driven to the plant. It’s like I open my eyes and immediately remember everything that’s waiting for me, but it’s not even only the work itself. It’s this… blank, sinking feeling, like there’s no good way for the day to go. I just lie there for a while thinking, “I can’t believe I have to do this again,” and it feels like I’m already behind, already failing, before anything has even happened.

Yes… at least at the beginning, definitely. I don’t know if I would’ve called it “happy” exactly, because it wasn’t like relaxed happiness, but I felt almost electrically upbeat. Like I had this charge running through me, and everything at the plant suddenly seemed obvious—what needed fixing, who needed to be called, which process should be changed first. It felt important, like I was finally operating at the speed the situation required.

And then pretty quickly that turned into this irritability. If someone questioned one of my ideas or wanted to slow down and review it, I’d feel this flash of frustration, like, “Why are you making this harder? Can’t you see this is the right move?” Even reasonable questions from other managers felt like they were dragging their feet or blocking something that was clearly important. At the time I thought I was just being decisive, but looking back, it was… more intense than that.

I just lie there for a while thinking, “I can’t believe I have to do this again,” and it feels like I’m already behind, already failing, before anything has even happened.\n\nDoctor: Does this heavy, hollow feeling in your chest come and go, or is it something you've been feeling almost all the time since things started to feel out of control at work?\n\nPatient: It’s been there almost all the time, honestly. Not every single second in exactly the same way, but… it’s like the background feeling now.\n\nLately I wake up in the temporary apartment and it’s already there before I’ve even done anything — this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is ruined before I’ve even gotten dressed or driven to the plant. It doesn’t feel like a normal “I don’t want to go to work” feeling. It’s more like I open my eyes and immediately feel sunk, like the whole day is already lost.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel extremely happy, excited, or on top of the world during the period when you were taking on a lot of projects at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… at least at the beginning, definitely. I don’t know if I would’ve called it “happy” exactly, because it wasn’t like relaxed happiness, but I felt almost electrically upbeat. Like I had this charge running through me, and everything at the plant suddenly seemed obvious—what needed fixing, who needed to be called, which process should be changed first. It felt important, like I was finally operating at the speed the situation required.\n\nAnd then pretty quickly that turned into this irritability. If someone questioned one of my ideas or wanted to slow down and review it, I’d feel this flash of frustration, like, “Why are you making this harder? Can’t you see this is the right move?” Even reasonable questions from other managers felt like they were dragging their feet or blocking something that was clearly important. At the time I thought I was just being decisive, but looking back, it was… more intense than that.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had other periods like this in the past where you felt extremely upbeat or irritable and took on a lot of projects at work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / industrial_production_manager / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Increased_Goal_Activity\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I went from launching nonstop plant redesign projects, calling suppliers after hours, and rewriting the shift plan by myself to suddenly being unable to keep up with the very plans I had set in motion.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
The clearest one is this stretch at the plant. I mean, that’s really why I came in, because it felt like I flipped into this mode where I was launching one redesign project after another, nonstop. I wasn’t just doing my normal workload — I was trying to overhaul parts of the plant layout, change processes, contact suppliers after hours, and then I’d go back and rewrite the shift plan myself because I felt like it had to be done right away and I couldn’t wait for everyone else to catch up.

At the time it felt productive, like I was finally fixing things. But then I had all these plans in motion — vendors waiting on answers, supervisors needing direction, people asking which version of the shift plan we were using — and suddenly I couldn’t keep up with the very things I’d started. That was the scary part. It wasn’t just ambition or being busy. It was like I created this whole storm of work and then got buried under it.

I’m trying to think back, and… nothing before this recent work situation stands out as clearly as what happened at the plant. This is really the thing that made me come in, because it wasn’t just that I was working hard or taking initiative.

I went from launching plant redesign projects nonstop, calling suppliers after hours, rewriting the shift plan by myself because I felt like I couldn’t wait for anyone else to do it — and it all felt urgent, like if I didn’t keep pushing then things would fall apart. And then somehow, pretty suddenly, I couldn’t keep up with the very plans I’d set in motion. There were too many moving pieces, too many people needing answers from me, and it was like I had created all of it but couldn’t hold it anymore.

So before this? I don’t know. Maybe I’ve had times where I’ve gotten very absorbed in work, but not like this. Not this nonstop, and not where it turned into me being buried under my own projects like that.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to separate them, honestly. It didn’t feel like two neat boxes, like, “Here’s the upbeat part,” and then later, “Here’s the hollow part.” At the start, I remember feeling really charged up and driven, and then the irritability came in pretty strongly when people slowed me down or questioned things.

The heavy, hollow feeling… I don’t know if I can place it exactly. I think it felt more like it showed up when I started realizing I couldn’t keep up with everything I’d started, but I’m not sure whether it was completely separate from the irritated, keyed-up feeling. There may have been overlap, but in my memory it’s kind of blurred together because the whole period felt so intense and out of control.

So I guess my honest answer is: I don’t know for sure. It felt like the energy and irritability came first, and the hollow heaviness was more around when things started catching up with me, but I can’t say it was perfectly one after the other.

It’s more like I open my eyes and immediately feel sunk, like the whole day is already lost.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel extremely happy, excited, or on top of the world during the period when you were taking on a lot of projects at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… at least at the beginning, definitely. It was like I created this whole storm of work and then got buried under it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced episodes like this, with periods of extreme productivity followed by feelings of being overwhelmed, at any other point in your life before this recent incident at work?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think back, and… nothing before this recent work situation stands out as clearly as what happened at the plant. It was like I created this whole storm of work and then got buried under it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced episodes like this, with periods of extreme productivity followed by feelings of being overwhelmed, at any other point in your life before this recent incident at work?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think back, and… nothing before this recent work situation stands out as clearly as what happened at the plant. This is really the thing that made me come in, because it wasn’t just that I was working hard or taking initiative.\n\nI went from launching plant redesign projects nonstop, calling suppliers after hours, rewriting the shift plan by myself because I felt like I couldn’t wait for anyone else to do it — and it all felt urgent, like if I didn’t keep pushing then things would fall apart. And then somehow, pretty suddenly, I couldn’t keep up with the very plans I’d set in motion. There were too many moving pieces, too many people needing answers from me, and it was like I had created all of it but couldn’t hold it anymore.\n\nSo before this? I don’t know. Maybe I’ve had times where I’ve gotten very absorbed in work, but not like this. Not this nonstop, and not where it turned into me being buried under my own projects like that.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience the extremely upbeat or irritable mood and the heavy, hollow feeling at the same time, or did they happen at different times?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate them, honestly. It didn’t feel like two neat boxes, like, “Here’s the upbeat part,” and then later, “Here’s the hollow part.” At the start, I remember feeling really charged up and driven, and then the irritability came in pretty strongly when people slowed me down or questioned things.\n\nThe heavy, hollow feeling… I don’t know if I can place it exactly. I think it felt more like it showed up when I started realizing I couldn’t keep up with everything I’d started, but I’m not sure whether it was completely separate from the irritated, keyed-up feeling. There may have been overlap, but in my memory it’s kind of blurred together because the whole period felt so intense and out of control.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I don’t know for sure. It felt like the energy and irritability came first, and the hollow heaviness was more around when things started catching up with me, but I can’t say it was perfectly one after the other.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you ever feel so good or hyper that you didn't need to sleep for a day or more during this period of extreme productivity?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / industrial_production_manager / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Decreased_Sleep\nmanifestation: \"I was sleeping maybe two or three hours and still showing up at the plant feeling sharp and charged, as if sleep was optional and kind of inefficient.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I went a full day with absolutely no sleep, but it got very close to feeling like sleep just didn’t matter. I was sleeping maybe two or three hours, and then I’d still show up at the plant feeling sharp, like unusually sharp, and charged up.

It wasn’t like normal exhaustion where you’re dragging yourself in and counting the hours until you can go home. I’d get up after barely sleeping and feel like, okay, good, I can get back to it now. Sleep felt optional, almost like it was inefficient or getting in the way of what I needed to do. At the time that seemed reasonable to me, but looking back, it was pretty strange.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I would’ve used the words “special powers” at the time, because that sounds almost cartoonish, but… yes, in the sense that I really believed I could do things that, looking back, were not realistic for one person.

I honestly thought I could fix the entire production workflow faster than the consulting team we had brought in. Not just contribute to it or manage it better — I mean I felt like I could see the whole system clearly and they were all moving too slowly. I remember thinking, almost with this certainty, that if everyone would just get out of my way, I could straighten the whole thing out myself.

And when people raised concerns, I didn’t really take them in. I brushed them off because failure didn’t feel possible to me. It wasn’t even like I was weighing the risks and deciding they were acceptable. It was more like the idea that I might be wrong just didn’t land. I felt… untouchable, I guess. Not magical, but like the normal limits didn’t apply to me in that moment.

like it takes effort just to pick up a binder, or I’m pacing the production office and picking at my nails because sitting still feels unbearable.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: In the shift-planning meeting I couldn’t hold the staffing numbers in my head long enough to make a basic decision, which is terrifying because that is normally exactly my job.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: At the beginning of this assignment I felt almost electrically upbeat and then weirdly irritable, like anyone questioning my ideas was slowing down something obvious and important.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: For a stretch I had this surplus of energy where I unpacked after midnight, reorganized the apartment, answered work emails, and still wanted to go back to the plant to check one more thing.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: I honestly believed I could fix the entire production workflow faster than the whole consulting team, and I brushed off concerns because failure didn’t even seem possible to me.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n  description: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: I was sleeping maybe two or three hours and still showing up at the plant feeling sharp and charged, as if sleep was optional and kind of inefficient.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: In meetings I could hear myself talking faster and longer than usual, jumping from line speeds to staffing to supplier issues before anyone could really respond.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I had to ask to be pulled out of a shift-planning meeting, and occupational health got involved because I wasn’t able to carry out basic production decisions my role depends on.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 3 weeks\n  manifestation: The intense shift from overdrive to barely functioning has been going on since I moved into the temporary apartment about three weeks ago.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 3 months\n  manifestation: The heavy depressed stretch has been with me for about three months, with the move and plant assignment making it much harder to manage.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / industrial_production_manager / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S040\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Did you experience the extremely upbeat or irritable mood and the heavy, hollow feeling at the same time, or did they happen at different times?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate them, honestly. It didn’t feel like two neat boxes, like, “Here’s the upbeat part,” and then later, “Here’s the hollow part.” At the start, I remember feeling really charged up and driven, and then the irritability came in pretty strongly when people slowed me down or questioned things.\n\nThe heavy, hollow feeling… I don’t know if I can place it exactly. I think it felt more like it showed up when I started realizing I couldn’t keep up with everything I’d started, but I’m not sure whether it was completely separate from the irritated, keyed-up feeling. There may have been overlap, but in my memory it’s kind of blurred together because the whole period felt so intense and out of control.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I don’t know for sure. It felt like the energy and irritability came first, and the hollow heaviness was more around when things started catching up with me, but I can’t say it was perfectly one after the other.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel so good or hyper that you didn't need to sleep for a day or more during this period of extreme productivity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I went a full day with absolutely no sleep, but it got very close to feeling like sleep just didn’t matter. I was sleeping maybe two or three hours, and then I’d still show up at the plant feeling sharp, like unusually sharp, and charged up.\n\nIt wasn’t like normal exhaustion where you’re dragging yourself in and counting the hours until you can go home. I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been able to see a clean pattern, like every few months or anything like that. It feels more sporadic to me, but I also don’t know if I’ve tracked it carefully enough to say that with confidence.

When I look back, I can remember certain stretches where everything ramped up and felt very intense, but I don’t think I could point to a calendar and say, “Oh, this happens at regular intervals.” It’s more like I realize afterward that something was off, and then I’m trying to piece it together from memory. It’s this… blank, sinking feeling, like there’s no good way for the day to go. I just lie there for a while thinking, “I can’t believe I have to do this again,” and it feels like I’m already behind, already failing, before anything has even happened.\n\nDoctor: Does this heavy, hollow feeling in your chest come and go, or is it something you've been feeling almost all the time since things started to feel out of control at work?\n\nPatient: It’s been there almost all the time, honestly. Not every single second in exactly the same way, but… it’s like the background feeling now.\n\nLately I wake up in the temporary apartment and it’s already there before I’ve even done anything — this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is ruined before I’ve even gotten dressed or driven to the plant. It doesn’t feel like a normal “I don’t want to go to work” feeling. It’s more like I open my eyes and immediately feel sunk, like the whole day is already lost.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel extremely happy, excited, or on top of the world during the period when you were taking on a lot of projects at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… at least at the beginning, definitely. I don’t know if I would’ve called it “happy” exactly, because it wasn’t like relaxed happiness, but I felt almost electrically upbeat. Like I had this charge running through me, and everything at the plant suddenly seemed obvious—what needed fixing, who needed to be called, which process should be changed first. It felt important, like I was finally operating at the speed the situation required.\n\nAnd then pretty quickly that turned into this irritability. If someone questioned one of my ideas or wanted to slow down and review it, I’d feel this flash of frustration, like, “Why are you making this harder? Can’t you see this is the right move?” Even reasonable questions from other managers felt like they were dragging their feet or blocking something that was clearly important. At the time I thought I was just being decisive, but looking back, it was… more intense than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had other periods like this in the past where you felt extremely upbeat or irritable and took on a lot of projects at work?\n\nPatient: The clearest one is this stretch at the plant. I mean, that’s really why I came in, because it felt like I flipped into this mode where I was launching one redesign project after another, nonstop. I wasn’t just doing my normal workload — I was trying to overhaul parts of the plant layout, change processes, contact suppliers after hours, and then I’d go back and rewrite the shift plan myself because I felt like it had to be done right away and I couldn’t wait for everyone else to catch up.\n\nAt the time it felt productive, like I was finally fixing things. But then I had all these plans in motion — vendors waiting on answers, supervisors needing direction, people asking which version of the shift plan we were using — and suddenly I couldn’t keep up with the very things I’d started. Yes… I think so. At the beginning of this assignment, especially, I felt almost electrically upbeat. That’s the best way I can describe it — like there was this current running through me and everything seemed clear and urgent and kind of exciting.

But it wasn’t just “happy,” exactly. It tipped into being really irritable too. If someone questioned one of my ideas or asked me to slow down, I’d get this immediate reaction like, Why are you blocking this? Why can’t you see this is obvious? It felt like they were dragging their feet on something important, and I had very little patience for it.

I don’t know if I was thinking of it as abnormal at the time, because in the moment it felt justified, like I was the only one seeing the situation clearly. But looking back, yes, my mood was definitely elevated and then weirdly sharp and irritable, and it lasted beyond just a day or two.

But then I had all these plans in motion — vendors waiting on answers, supervisors needing direction, people asking which version of the shift plan we were using — and suddenly I couldn’t keep up with the very things I’d started. That was the scary part. It wasn’t just ambition or being busy. It was like I created this whole storm of work and then got buried under it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced episodes like this, with periods of extreme productivity followed by feelings of being overwhelmed, at any other point in your life before this recent incident at work?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think back, and… nothing before this recent work situation stands out as clearly as what happened at the plant. This is really the thing that made me come in, because it wasn’t just that I was working hard or taking initiative.\n\nI went from launching plant redesign projects nonstop, calling suppliers after hours, rewriting the shift plan by myself because I felt like I couldn’t wait for anyone else to do it — and it all felt urgent, like if I didn’t keep pushing then things would fall apart. And then somehow, pretty suddenly, I couldn’t keep up with the very plans I’d set in motion. There were too many moving pieces, too many people needing answers from me, and it was like I had created all of it but couldn’t hold it anymore.\n\nSo before this? I don’t know. Maybe I’ve had times where I’ve gotten very absorbed in work, but not like this. Not this nonstop, and not where it turned into me being buried under my own projects like that.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience the extremely upbeat or irritable mood and the heavy, hollow feeling at the same time, or did they happen at different times?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate them, honestly. It didn’t feel like two neat boxes, like, “Here’s the upbeat part,” and then later, “Here’s the hollow part.” At the start, I remember feeling really charged up and driven, and then the irritability came in pretty strongly when people slowed me down or questioned things.\n\nThe heavy, hollow feeling… I don’t know if I can place it exactly. I think it felt more like it showed up when I started realizing I couldn’t keep up with everything I’d started, but I’m not sure whether it was completely separate from the irritated, keyed-up feeling. There may have been overlap, but in my memory it’s kind of blurred together because the whole period felt so intense and out of control.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I don’t know for sure. It felt like the energy and irritability came first, and the hollow heaviness was more around when things started catching up with me, but I can’t say it was perfectly one after the other.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel so good or hyper that you didn't need to sleep for a day or more during this period of extreme productivity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I went a full day with absolutely no sleep, but it got very close to feeling like sleep just didn’t matter. I was sleeping maybe two or three hours, and then I’d still show up at the plant feeling sharp, like unusually sharp, and charged up.\n\nIt wasn’t like normal exhaustion where you’re dragging yourself in and counting the hours until you can go home. I’d get up after barely sleeping and feel like, okay, good, I can get back to it now. Sleep felt optional, almost like it was inefficient or getting in the way of what I needed to do. At the time that seemed reasonable to me, but looking back, it was pretty strange.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel like you were invincible or had special powers during this period of extreme productivity?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I would’ve used the words “special powers” at the time, because that sounds almost cartoonish, but… yes, in the sense that I really believed I could do things that, looking back, were not realistic for one person.\n\nI honestly thought I could fix the entire production workflow faster than the consulting team we had brought in. Not just contribute to it or manage it better — I mean I felt like I could see the whole system clearly and they were all moving too slowly. I remember thinking, almost with this certainty, that if everyone would just get out of my way, I could straighten the whole thing out myself.\n\nAnd when people raised concerns, I didn’t really take them in. I brushed them off because failure didn’t feel possible to me. It wasn’t even like I was weighing the risks and deciding they were acceptable. It was more like the idea that I might be wrong just didn’t land. I felt… untouchable, I guess. Not magical, but like the normal limits didn’t apply to me in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of extreme productivity and mood changes happened at fairly regular intervals, or have they been more sporadic?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been able to see a clean pattern, like every few months or anything like that. It feels more sporadic to me, but I also don’t know if I’ve tracked it carefully enough to say that with confidence.\n\nWhen I look back, I can remember certain stretches where everything ramped up and felt very intense, but I don’t think I could point to a calendar and say, “Oh, this happens at regular intervals.” It’s more like I realize afterward that something was off, and then I’m trying to piece it together from memory. So… I’d say I haven’t noticed a regular cycle, but I’m not completely sure.\n\nDoctor: How long did these periods of extreme productivity typically last?\n\nPatient: The current one has been going on for about three weeks, I think. It really seemed to start around when I moved into the temporary apartment. Since then it’s been this intense swing between being in overdrive and then barely functioning.\n\nIt’s hard for me to separate it into neat “episodes,” because while I’m in it, it feels like I’m just reacting to what has to be done. But looking back, from the move into that place until now, it’s been about three weeks of not feeling like myself — like I’m either pushing way past normal or then dropping off completely. So… typically, at least this time, it’s lasted around three weeks.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any periods of abnormally elevated or irritable mood that lasted for at least one week?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. At the beginning of this assignment, especially, I felt almost electrically upbeat. That’s the best way I can describe it — like there was this current running through me and everything seemed clear and urgent and kind of exciting.\n\nBut it wasn’t just “happy,” exactly. It tipped into being really irritable too. If someone questioned one of my ideas or asked me to slow down, I’d get this immediate reaction like, Why are you blocking this? Why can’t you see this is obvious? It felt like they were dragging their feet on something important, and I had very little patience for it.\n\nI don’t know if I was thinking of it as abnormal at the time, because in the moment it felt justified, like I was the only one seeing the situation clearly. But looking back, yes, my mood was definitely elevated and then weirdly sharp and irritable, and it lasted beyond just a day or two.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

That’s the best way I can describe it — like there was this current running through me and everything seemed clear and urgent and kind of exciting.\n\nBut it wasn’t just “happy,” exactly. It tipped into being really irritable too. If someone questioned one of my ideas or asked me to slow down, I’d get this immediate reaction like, Why are you blocking this? Why can’t you see this is obvious? It felt like they were dragging their feet on something important, and I had very little patience for it.\n\nI don’t know if I was thinking of it as abnormal at the time, because in the moment it felt justified, like I was the only one seeing the situation clearly. But looking back, yes, my mood was definitely elevated and then weirdly sharp and irritable, and it lasted beyond just a day or two.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these episodes affected your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / industrial_production_manager / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I had to ask to be pulled out of a shift-planning meeting, and occupational health got involved because I wasn’t able to carry out basic production decisions my role depends on.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. The biggest place it’s shown up is at work, honestly. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like one awkward meeting and that was it, because it felt more serious than that.

There was a shift-planning meeting where I got so overwhelmed and unable to function in the way I’m supposed to that I actually had to ask to be pulled out of it. And that’s… not normal for me. My job depends on being able to make production decisions, prioritize, weigh things up quickly, and I just couldn’t do it. I couldn’t carry out even basic decisions that are usually just part of the role.

It escalated to the point that occupational health got involved, which felt really humiliating and scary. Like, it wasn’t just me privately struggling or having a bad day — other people could see that I wasn’t able to do what I needed to do at work. So yes, it’s definitely affected coworkers and my functioning there, at least.

But then I had all these plans in motion — vendors waiting on answers, supervisors needing direction, people asking which version of the shift plan we were using — and suddenly I couldn’t keep up with the very things I’d started. That was the scary part. It wasn’t just ambition or being busy. It was like I created this whole storm of work and then got buried under it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced episodes like this, with periods of extreme productivity followed by feelings of being overwhelmed, at any other point in your life before this recent incident at work?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think back, and… nothing before this recent work situation stands out as clearly as what happened at the plant. This is really the thing that made me come in, because it wasn’t just that I was working hard or taking initiative.\n\nI went from launching plant redesign projects nonstop, calling suppliers after hours, rewriting the shift plan by myself because I felt like I couldn’t wait for anyone else to do it — and it all felt urgent, like if I didn’t keep pushing then things would fall apart. And then somehow, pretty suddenly, I couldn’t keep up with the very plans I’d set in motion. There were too many moving pieces, too many people needing answers from me, and it was like I had created all of it but couldn’t hold it anymore.\n\nSo before this? I don’t know. Maybe I’ve had times where I’ve gotten very absorbed in work, but not like this. Not this nonstop, and not where it turned into me being buried under my own projects like that.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience the extremely upbeat or irritable mood and the heavy, hollow feeling at the same time, or did they happen at different times?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate them, honestly. It didn’t feel like two neat boxes, like, “Here’s the upbeat part,” and then later, “Here’s the hollow part.” At the start, I remember feeling really charged up and driven, and then the irritability came in pretty strongly when people slowed me down or questioned things.\n\nThe heavy, hollow feeling… I don’t know if I can place it exactly. I think it felt more like it showed up when I started realizing I couldn’t keep up with everything I’d started, but I’m not sure whether it was completely separate from the irritated, keyed-up feeling. There may have been overlap, but in my memory it’s kind of blurred together because the whole period felt so intense and out of control.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I don’t know for sure. It felt like the energy and irritability came first, and the hollow heaviness was more around when things started catching up with me, but I can’t say it was perfectly one after the other.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel so good or hyper that you didn't need to sleep for a day or more during this period of extreme productivity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I went a full day with absolutely no sleep, but it got very close to feeling like sleep just didn’t matter. I was sleeping maybe two or three hours, and then I’d still show up at the plant feeling sharp, like unusually sharp, and charged up.\n\nIt wasn’t like normal exhaustion where you’re dragging yourself in and counting the hours until you can go home. I brushed them off because failure didn’t feel possible to me. It wasn’t even like I was weighing the risks and deciding they were acceptable. There were too many moving pieces, too many people needing answers from me, and it was like I had created all of it but couldn’t hold it anymore.\n\nSo before this? I don’t know. Maybe I’ve had times where I’ve gotten very absorbed in work, but not like this. Not this nonstop, and not where it turned into me being buried under my own projects like that.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience the extremely upbeat or irritable mood and the heavy, hollow feeling at the same time, or did they happen at different times?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate them, honestly. It’s this… blank, sinking feeling, like there’s no good way for the day to go. I just lie there for a while thinking, “I can’t believe I have to do this again,” and it feels like I’m already behind, already failing, before anything has even happened.\n\nDoctor: Does this heavy, hollow feeling in your chest come and go, or is it something you've been feeling almost all the time since things started to feel out of control at work?\n\nPatient: It’s been there almost all the time, honestly. Not every single second in exactly the same way, but… it’s like the background feeling now.\n\nLately I wake up in the temporary apartment and it’s already there before I’ve even done anything — this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is ruined before I’ve even gotten dressed or driven to the plant. It doesn’t feel like a normal “I don’t want to go to work” feeling. It’s more like I open my eyes and immediately feel sunk, like the whole day is already lost.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel extremely happy, excited, or on top of the world during the period when you were taking on a lot of projects at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… at least at the beginning, definitely. I don’t know if I would’ve called it “happy” exactly, because it wasn’t like relaxed happiness, but I felt almost electrically upbeat. Like I had this charge running through me, and everything at the plant suddenly seemed obvious—what needed fixing, who needed to be called, which process should be changed first. It felt important, like I was finally operating at the speed the situation required.\n\nAnd then pretty quickly that turned into this irritability. If someone questioned one of my ideas or wanted to slow down and review it, I’d feel this flash of frustration, like, “Why are you making this harder? Can’t you see this is the right move?” Even reasonable questions from other managers felt like they were dragging their feet or blocking something that was clearly important. At the time I thought I was just being decisive, but looking back, it was… more intense than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had other periods like this in the past where you felt extremely upbeat or irritable and took on a lot of projects at work?\n\nPatient: The clearest one is this stretch at the plant. I mean, that’s really why I came in, because it felt like I flipped into this mode where I was launching one redesign project after another, nonstop. I wasn’t just doing my normal workload — I was trying to overhaul parts of the plant layout, change processes, contact suppliers after hours, and then I’d go back and rewrite the shift plan myself because I felt like it had to be done right away and I couldn’t wait for everyone else to catch up.\n\nAt the time it felt productive, like I was finally fixing things. But then I had all these plans in motion — vendors waiting on answers, supervisors needing direction, people asking which version of the shift plan we were using — and suddenly I couldn’t keep up with the very things I’d started. That was the scary part. It wasn’t just ambition or being busy. It was like I created this whole storm of work and then got buried under it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced episodes like this, with periods of extreme productivity followed by feelings of being overwhelmed, at any other point in your life before this recent incident at work?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think back, and… nothing before this recent work situation stands out as clearly as what happened at the plant. This is really the thing that made me come in, because it wasn’t just that I was working hard or taking initiative.\n\nI went from launching plant redesign projects nonstop, calling suppliers after hours, rewriting the shift plan by myself because I felt like I couldn’t wait for anyone else to do it — and it all felt urgent, like if I didn’t keep pushing then things would fall apart. And then somehow, pretty suddenly, I couldn’t keep up with the very plans I’d set in motion. There were too many moving pieces, too many people needing answers from me, and it was like I had created all of it but couldn’t hold it anymore.\n\nSo before this? I don’t know. Maybe I’ve had times where I’ve gotten very absorbed in work, but not like this. Not this nonstop, and not where it turned into me being buried under my own projects like that.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience the extremely upbeat or irritable mood and the heavy, hollow feeling at the same time, or did they happen at different times?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate them, honestly. It didn’t feel like two neat boxes, like, “Here’s the upbeat part,” and then later, “Here’s the hollow part.” At the start, I remember feeling really charged up and driven, and then the irritability came in pretty strongly when people slowed me down or questioned things.\n\nThe heavy, hollow feeling… I don’t know if I can place it exactly. I think it felt more like it showed up when I started realizing I couldn’t keep up with everything I’d started, but I’m not sure whether it was completely separate from the irritated, keyed-up feeling. There may have been overlap, but in my memory it’s kind of blurred together because the whole period felt so intense and out of control.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I don’t know for sure. It felt like the energy and irritability came first, and the hollow heaviness was more around when things started catching up with me, but I can’t say it was perfectly one after the other.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel so good or hyper that you didn't need to sleep for a day or more during this period of extreme productivity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I went a full day with absolutely no sleep, but it got very close to feeling like sleep just didn’t matter. I was sleeping maybe two or three hours, and then I’d still show up at the plant feeling sharp, like unusually sharp, and charged up.\n\nIt wasn’t like normal exhaustion where you’re dragging yourself in and counting the hours until you can go home. I’d get up after barely sleeping and feel like, okay, good, I can get back to it now. Sleep felt optional, almost like it was inefficient or getting in the way of what I needed to do. At the time that seemed reasonable to me, but looking back, it was pretty strange.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel like you were invincible or had special powers during this period of extreme productivity?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I would’ve used the words “special powers” at the time, because that sounds almost cartoonish, but… yes, in the sense that I really believed I could do things that, looking back, were not realistic for one person.\n\nI honestly thought I could fix the entire production workflow faster than the consulting team we had brought in. Not just contribute to it or manage it better — I mean I felt like I could see the whole system clearly and they were all moving too slowly. I remember thinking, almost with this certainty, that if everyone would just get out of my way, I could straighten the whole thing out myself.\n\nAnd when people raised concerns, I didn’t really take them in. I brushed them off because failure didn’t feel possible to me. It wasn’t even like I was weighing the risks and deciding they were acceptable. It was more like the idea that I might be wrong just didn’t land. I felt… untouchable, I guess. Not magical, but like the normal limits didn’t apply to me in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of extreme productivity and mood changes happened at fairly regular intervals, or have they been more sporadic?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been able to see a clean pattern, like every few months or anything like that. It feels more sporadic to me, but I also don’t know if I’ve tracked it carefully enough to say that with confidence.\n\nWhen I look back, I can remember certain stretches where everything ramped up and felt very intense, but I don’t think I could point to a calendar and say, “Oh, this happens at regular intervals.” It’s more like I realize afterward that something was off, and then I’m trying to piece it together from memory. So… I’d say I haven’t noticed a regular cycle, but I’m not completely sure.\n\nDoctor: How long did these periods of extreme productivity typically last?\n\nPatient: The current one has been going on for about three weeks, I think. It really seemed to start around when I moved into the temporary apartment. Since then it’s been this intense swing between being in overdrive and then barely functioning.\n\nIt’s hard for me to separate it into neat “episodes,” because while I’m in it, it feels like I’m just reacting to what has to be done. But looking back, from the move into that place until now, it’s been about three weeks of not feeling like myself — like I’m either pushing way past normal or then dropping off completely. So… typically, at least this time, it’s lasted around three weeks.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any periods of abnormally elevated or irritable mood that lasted for at least one week?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. At the beginning of this assignment, especially, I felt almost electrically upbeat. That’s the best way I can describe it — like there was this current running through me and everything seemed clear and urgent and kind of exciting.\n\nBut it wasn’t just “happy,” exactly. It tipped into being really irritable too. If someone questioned one of my ideas or asked me to slow down, I’d get this immediate reaction like, Why are you blocking this? Why can’t you see this is obvious? It felt like they were dragging their feet on something important, and I had very little patience for it.\n\nI don’t know if I was thinking of it as abnormal at the time, because in the moment it felt justified, like I was the only one seeing the situation clearly. But looking back, yes, my mood was definitely elevated and then weirdly sharp and irritable, and it lasted beyond just a day or two.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes affected your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yes. The biggest place it’s shown up is at work, honestly. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like one awkward meeting and that was it, because it felt more serious than that.\n\nThere was a shift-planning meeting where I got so overwhelmed and unable to function in the way I’m supposed to that I actually had to ask to be pulled out of it. And that’s… not normal for me. My job depends on being able to make production decisions, prioritize, weigh things up quickly, and I just couldn’t do it. I couldn’t carry out even basic decisions that are usually just part of the role.\n\nIt escalated to the point that occupational health got involved, which felt really humiliating and scary. Like, it wasn’t just me privately struggling or having a bad day — other people could see that I wasn’t able to do what I needed to do at work. So yes, it’s definitely affected coworkers and my functioning there, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these episodes of extreme productivity and mood changes tend to happen at certain times of the year?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I’ve tried to think about that, because it would be helpful if there were some obvious pattern, like “oh, this happens every winter” or “it always happens in spring,” but I can’t say I’ve noticed that clearly.\n\nIt feels more tied to whatever is going on in my life at the time than to the calendar, if that makes sense. But even saying that, I’m not sure if I’m just missing a pattern because I’m only really looking back now. When I’m in it, I’m not exactly keeping track in a neat way — I’m usually just caught up in it and trying to get through whatever is happening.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed a specific time of year. Not that I could confidently point to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I keep going back over things in my head, trying to work out whether this was the first “real” time or whether I’ve had smaller versions before and just didn’t label them that way.

At the time, I don’t think I would have said, “Oh, this is an episode.” I probably would’ve explained it as being stressed, or under pressure, or just getting really invested in something. So it’s hard to separate what was actually unusual from what I only recognize now because the work situation became so obvious and hard to ignore.

I can say this recent one at work felt like the clearest and most disruptive one. That’s the one where other people noticed and where it had consequences I couldn’t really brush off. Before that… I don’t know. There may have been times where I was more intense or more driven than usual, but I can’t confidently say they were the same thing. I don’t want to overstate it and give you a neat answer if I’m honestly not sure.

Not every single second in exactly the same way, but… it’s like the background feeling now.\n\nLately I wake up in the temporary apartment and it’s already there before I’ve even done anything — this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like the day is ruined before I’ve even gotten dressed or driven to the plant. It doesn’t feel like a normal “I don’t want to go to work” feeling. It’s more like I open my eyes and immediately feel sunk, like the whole day is already lost.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel extremely happy, excited, or on top of the world during the period when you were taking on a lot of projects at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… at least at the beginning, definitely. I don’t know if I would’ve called it “happy” exactly, because it wasn’t like relaxed happiness, but I felt almost electrically upbeat. Like I had this charge running through me, and everything at the plant suddenly seemed obvious—what needed fixing, who needed to be called, which process should be changed first. It felt important, like I was finally operating at the speed the situation required.\n\nAnd then pretty quickly that turned into this irritability. If someone questioned one of my ideas or wanted to slow down and review it, I’d feel this flash of frustration, like, “Why are you making this harder? Can’t you see this is the right move?” Even reasonable questions from other managers felt like they were dragging their feet or blocking something that was clearly important. At the time I thought I was just being decisive, but looking back, it was… more intense than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had other periods like this in the past where you felt extremely upbeat or irritable and took on a lot of projects at work?\n\nPatient: The clearest one is this stretch at the plant. I mean, that’s really why I came in, because it felt like I flipped into this mode where I was launching one redesign project after another, nonstop. I wasn’t just doing my normal workload — I was trying to overhaul parts of the plant layout, change processes, contact suppliers after hours, and then I’d go back and rewrite the shift plan myself because I felt like it had to be done right away and I couldn’t wait for everyone else to catch up.\n\nAt the time it felt productive, like I was finally fixing things. But then I had all these plans in motion — vendors waiting on answers, supervisors needing direction, people asking which version of the shift plan we were using — and suddenly I couldn’t keep up with the very things I’d started. That was the scary part. It wasn’t just ambition or being busy. It was like I created this whole storm of work and then got buried under it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced episodes like this, with periods of extreme productivity followed by feelings of being overwhelmed, at any other point in your life before this recent incident at work?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think back, and… nothing before this recent work situation stands out as clearly as what happened at the plant. This is really the thing that made me come in, because it wasn’t just that I was working hard or taking initiative.\n\nI went from launching plant redesign projects nonstop, calling suppliers after hours, rewriting the shift plan by myself because I felt like I couldn’t wait for anyone else to do it — and it all felt urgent, like if I didn’t keep pushing then things would fall apart. And then somehow, pretty suddenly, I couldn’t keep up with the very plans I’d set in motion. There were too many moving pieces, too many people needing answers from me, and it was like I had created all of it but couldn’t hold it anymore.\n\nSo before this? I don’t know. Maybe I’ve had times where I’ve gotten very absorbed in work, but not like this. Not this nonstop, and not where it turned into me being buried under my own projects like that.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience the extremely upbeat or irritable mood and the heavy, hollow feeling at the same time, or did they happen at different times?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate them, honestly. It didn’t feel like two neat boxes, like, “Here’s the upbeat part,” and then later, “Here’s the hollow part.” At the start, I remember feeling really charged up and driven, and then the irritability came in pretty strongly when people slowed me down or questioned things.\n\nThe heavy, hollow feeling… I don’t know if I can place it exactly. I think it felt more like it showed up when I started realizing I couldn’t keep up with everything I’d started, but I’m not sure whether it was completely separate from the irritated, keyed-up feeling. There may have been overlap, but in my memory it’s kind of blurred together because the whole period felt so intense and out of control.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I don’t know for sure. It felt like the energy and irritability came first, and the hollow heaviness was more around when things started catching up with me, but I can’t say it was perfectly one after the other.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel so good or hyper that you didn't need to sleep for a day or more during this period of extreme productivity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I went a full day with absolutely no sleep, but it got very close to feeling like sleep just didn’t matter. I was sleeping maybe two or three hours, and then I’d still show up at the plant feeling sharp, like unusually sharp, and charged up.\n\nIt wasn’t like normal exhaustion where you’re dragging yourself in and counting the hours until you can go home. I’d get up after barely sleeping and feel like, okay, good, I can get back to it now. Sleep felt optional, almost like it was inefficient or getting in the way of what I needed to do. At the time that seemed reasonable to me, but looking back, it was pretty strange.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel like you were invincible or had special powers during this period of extreme productivity?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I would’ve used the words “special powers” at the time, because that sounds almost cartoonish, but… yes, in the sense that I really believed I could do things that, looking back, were not realistic for one person.\n\nI honestly thought I could fix the entire production workflow faster than the consulting team we had brought in. Not just contribute to it or manage it better — I mean I felt like I could see the whole system clearly and they were all moving too slowly. I remember thinking, almost with this certainty, that if everyone would just get out of my way, I could straighten the whole thing out myself.\n\nAnd when people raised concerns, I didn’t really take them in. I brushed them off because failure didn’t feel possible to me. It wasn’t even like I was weighing the risks and deciding they were acceptable. It was more like the idea that I might be wrong just didn’t land. I felt… untouchable, I guess. Not magical, but like the normal limits didn’t apply to me in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of extreme productivity and mood changes happened at fairly regular intervals, or have they been more sporadic?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been able to see a clean pattern, like every few months or anything like that. It feels more sporadic to me, but I also don’t know if I’ve tracked it carefully enough to say that with confidence.\n\nWhen I look back, I can remember certain stretches where everything ramped up and felt very intense, but I don’t think I could point to a calendar and say, “Oh, this happens at regular intervals.” It’s more like I realize afterward that something was off, and then I’m trying to piece it together from memory. So… I’d say I haven’t noticed a regular cycle, but I’m not completely sure.\n\nDoctor: How long did these periods of extreme productivity typically last?\n\nPatient: The current one has been going on for about three weeks, I think. It really seemed to start around when I moved into the temporary apartment. Since then it’s been this intense swing between being in overdrive and then barely functioning.\n\nIt’s hard for me to separate it into neat “episodes,” because while I’m in it, it feels like I’m just reacting to what has to be done. But looking back, from the move into that place until now, it’s been about three weeks of not feeling like myself — like I’m either pushing way past normal or then dropping off completely. So… typically, at least this time, it’s lasted around three weeks.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any periods of abnormally elevated or irritable mood that lasted for at least one week?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. At the beginning of this assignment, especially, I felt almost electrically upbeat. That’s the best way I can describe it — like there was this current running through me and everything seemed clear and urgent and kind of exciting.\n\nBut it wasn’t just “happy,” exactly. It tipped into being really irritable too. If someone questioned one of my ideas or asked me to slow down, I’d get this immediate reaction like, Why are you blocking this? Why can’t you see this is obvious? It felt like they were dragging their feet on something important, and I had very little patience for it.\n\nI don’t know if I was thinking of it as abnormal at the time, because in the moment it felt justified, like I was the only one seeing the situation clearly. But looking back, yes, my mood was definitely elevated and then weirdly sharp and irritable, and it lasted beyond just a day or two.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes affected your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yes. The biggest place it’s shown up is at work, honestly. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like one awkward meeting and that was it, because it felt more serious than that.\n\nThere was a shift-planning meeting where I got so overwhelmed and unable to function in the way I’m supposed to that I actually had to ask to be pulled out of it. And that’s… not normal for me. My job depends on being able to make production decisions, prioritize, weigh things up quickly, and I just couldn’t do it. I couldn’t carry out even basic decisions that are usually just part of the role.\n\nIt escalated to the point that occupational health got involved, which felt really humiliating and scary. Like, it wasn’t just me privately struggling or having a bad day — other people could see that I wasn’t able to do what I needed to do at work. So yes, it’s definitely affected coworkers and my functioning there, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these episodes of extreme productivity and mood changes tend to happen at certain times of the year?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I’ve tried to think about that, because it would be helpful if there were some obvious pattern, like “oh, this happens every winter” or “it always happens in spring,” but I can’t say I’ve noticed that clearly.\n\nIt feels more tied to whatever is going on in my life at the time than to the calendar, if that makes sense. But even saying that, I’m not sure if I’m just missing a pattern because I’m only really looking back now. When I’m in it, I’m not exactly keeping track in a neat way — I’m usually just caught up in it and trying to get through whatever is happening.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed a specific time of year. Not that I could confidently point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced episodes like this before the recent one at work, or is this the first time?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I keep going back over things in my head, trying to work out whether this was the first “real” time or whether I’ve had smaller versions before and just didn’t label them that way.\n\nAt the time, I don’t think I would have said, “Oh, this is an episode.” I probably would’ve explained it as being stressed, or under pressure, or just getting really invested in something. So it’s hard to separate what was actually unusual from what I only recognize now because the work situation became so obvious and hard to ignore.\n\nI can say this recent one at work felt like the clearest and most disruptive one. That’s the one where other people noticed and where it had consequences I couldn’t really brush off. Before that… I don’t know. There may have been times where I was more intense or more driven than usual, but I can’t confidently say they were the same thing. I don’t want to overstate it and give you a neat answer if I’m honestly not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I honestly don’t know how to count it. I’m sorry, that’s probably not the clean answer you’re looking for, but I don’t feel confident saying “yes, definitely more than one” in the same way as the work one.
